Why the Cyberpocalypse Might Hit China Harder

The geopolitics of disclosure norms and hacker incentives

Chang Che

Nov 8

Guest post

READ IN APP

Chang Che is a writer and contributor to the New Yorker.

In August, researchers at a California security firm used an OpenAI model to find a software bug in PHP, a programming language that undergird some seventy per cent of the world’s websites, including Wikipedia and WordPress. They alerted PHP’s developers, who quietly began working on a fix. By late October, the fix was ready, but before they could alert the world, the break-in attempts had begun. Days earlier, Amazon detected someone trying to hack into its servers; attacks code began circulating online. Some governments and companies began pulling websites down, rather than risk a security breach. One security firm described the flaws as some of the worst ever found in PHP, “and arguably in software history.”

Before AI, the internet might have looked, to a hacker, like a worn-out bug screen. You could discern some holes with a keen eye, and that knowledge was reliably asymmetric—others probably didn’t know about the holes you found. Moreover, tunneling through the holes undetected took months or years of human labor, talent, and money. Now, with AI, the internet looks more like a block of Swiss cheese: the holes are obvious, anyone can, or will, see them, and the time and cost it takes to exploit these software flaws have plunged alarmingly fast. According to Google’s incident response team, the average time it takes for cyber criminals to exploit a new vulnerability is -7 days, meaning hacking attempts are “routinely occurring before a patch is even released.”

A China Central Television (CCTV) feature about the PHP vulnerability discovered by cybersecurity firm Calif, with the aid of OpenAI models. Source.

Hackers refer to unbeknownst software bugs like PHP as “zero-days” because a defender has had zero days to protect themselves upon its discovery. Once a hacker discovers a zero-day in, say, iOS software, every iPhone user is potentially vulnerable to a hack until Apple finds out about it, fixes it, releases a new patch, and, crucially, gets customers to install the software update. Beyond losing your crypto-wallets, there is the question of what the cyberpocalypse means, strategically, for the United States and China. According to the data tracking site Censys, the two countries reacted at drastically different speeds to the PHP zero-day. One week after its public disclosure, the United States had successfully patched over a quarter of the 14 million services exposed to the flaw. (Japan had about the same percentage.) China, by contrast, had patched an abysmal 4% of its potentially vulnerable services.

ChinaTalk is a reader-supported publication. To receive new posts and support our work, consider becoming a free or paid subscriber.

Upgrade to paid

Why was China, who spent a decade building up a formidable cybersecurity governance system, so slow to patch one of the biggest software flaws in history? Until now, the cybersecurity industry was largely built around finding flaws. Companies buy them from hackers, and governments hoard them to spy on their enemies. An industry built around bug-discovery seems to resemble the AI race or the Manhattan Project: it’s a question of how a state can marshall a small elite group toward a specific goal. China is very good at the former—as it has repeatedly demonstrated with AI, semiconductors, batteries, robotics.

We are now moving from an era of zero-day scarcity to zero-day abundance. Bugs that Washington, Moscow, and Beijing used to hoard like nuclear fission material are now becoming available to an educated AI user. An era of zero-day abundance will force cybersecurity to shift to an industry built around fixing flaws as fast as humanly, or superhumanly, possible. This is a challenge for the many, not the few—it demands something from your average IT person at a school, the contractor running a local water plant, a security team at a tech firm whose warnings may have previously been ignored. It’s almost a test of the metabolism of a society: how well bottom-up incentives are working. China, with its Leninist governance system, may have trouble in an era that demands such uncoordinated, yet collective, actions.

Hayek and the birth of disclosure norms

“Zero-days,” unlike proofs, can be weaponized, and in the 1990s, there were practically no rules for what one should do with the ingredients of a potential cyber-nuke. “White hat” hackers—people who hack to help shore up defenses—who told companies about their flaws were often ignored, bullied, or threatened with lawsuits. So hackers began posting the flaws publicly. This forced companies to fix them, but it also handed “black hat” hackers the cyber equivalent of a bank’s blueprint, so a deal had to be brokered. Entire books have been written about the battle between the hacker community and tech companies, but the upshot was that by the 2010s, an equilibrium solidified. Under the rules of “coordinated vulnerability disclosure,” a researcher notifies the affected company first, gives them time to fix it, then tells the world.

Today, a web of institutions and standards, anchored in the United States, underwrite these norms. Google’s Project Zero, an elite bug-hunting team, gives software makers 90 days to fix a flaw before it goes public. The “bug bounty” program, which Netscape pioneered in 1995, went from a quirky experiment to an industry standard that can pay hackers six figures for a single flaw. Serious bugs get an ID number from CVE, a catalog run by the US government. What’s striking is that no one designed this system from the top-down. It more closely resembled Friedrich Hayek’s theory of “spontaneous order”: free, commercially motivated groups—hackers and companies—adjusted to each other and the rules and institutions emerged. Hackers wanted credit and rewards; firms needed time. Money supplied the incentives for a bug-patching ecology that resulted in a more secure web.

One way to measure how American this metabolic process is is to compare the maturity of bug bounty programs in other countries. Out of the ten largest tech companies in the United States, nine of them have bug bounty programs, according to a 2026 study; in China, the number is six out of ten. (The firms that have them, like Alibaba, are international facing, while the ones that don’t are state-owned like China Mobile.) The pay scale is incomparable. Apple and Google can pay millions of dollars for critical zero-day flaws, while top Chinese programs like Tencent top out at $15,000. The West underwrites the ecosystem for patching up the internet.

After Snowden

Of course, this state of affairs didn’t last. While the bug-disclosure norms were taking shape, there was also a parallel, secretive cyber-arms-trade going on. In This Is How They Tell Me the World Ends, published in 2021, the former New York Times cybersecurity reporter Nicole Perlroth shows how the NSA was secretly buying zero-days from hackers to spy on American citizens and the world. Hackers were paid life-changing sums to keep their flaws secret, and the exposure of this hoarding by the Snowden leaks helped break up the love affair between Chinese hackers and Western bug bounty programs.

Edward Snowden. Source: Wikimedia Commons

The Snowden revelations in 2013 catalyzed China’s cybersecurity arms race. In 2014, Xi Jinping took charge of a newly empowered cybersecurity division. Beijing began to pass laws to de-Americanize China’s tech stack, and state-sponsored cyberattacks on the United States skyrocketed. Among the biggest changes China implemented was to treat zero-days like national security assets. In 2018, Chinese hackers abruptly disappeared from the international hacking contests they once dominated. Contributions by Chinese researchers to Microsoft bug bounty programs plummeted in 2020. A year later, in 2021, Beijing passed a law that prohibited Chinese security researchers from publicizing zero-day flaws or passing them to a foreign bug-broker. They can report the bug to the company that runs the software or to China’s Ministry of Industry and Information Technology (MIIT). Any company in China that learns of a flaw has 48 hours to tell the ministry.

For decades, Western tech companies and white hat hackers had created a bug-fixing ecosystem that prized openness and defense. Chinese hackers participated, not least for the money and fame, but because fixing those bugs hardly felt “western” when Chinese offices ran Windows and Chinese phones ran Android. But it’s understandable why—in light of the NSA’s resolve to enlist US tech companies as a “backdoor”—China felt queasy about watching their hackers patch Microsoft’s bugs.

The NSA’s security rationale had a name: “nobody but us,” or NOBUS. Deliberately keeping the internet vulnerable was acceptable, in this view, as long as no other country knew about those vulnerabilities and could exploit it. In sourcing and keeping zero-days a secret for cyberoffensive capabilities, the US government undermined the global disclosure system its own society midwifed. China’s cybersecurity laws were the response to NOBUS. The message was: “if you are going to hoard zero-days to spy on us, we’re going to do the same.” Several experts described the 2021 law to me as a “vacuum” that siphons zero-days to what researchers at the Atlantic Council, a D.C.-based think tank, found is a national database visible to China’s spy agency. The rules were put to the test less than three months later, in 2021, when an Alibaba researcher found one of the worst software flaws in a decade in a free code base maintained by the Apache Software Foundation, a US-based nonprofit. He reported it to Apache as global norms prescribe, and MIIT suspended a partnership with Alibaba as punishment.

China used the force of law to compel its hackers to replicate the NSA’s zero-day arsenal, and it seems to have worked. Perlroth told me that after the zero-day vacuum law, the number of zero-day exploits from China-linked hackers exploded. “China was using their authoritarianism and these new laws to their advantage,” she told me. “It was an incredible up-leveling.” But what it couldn’t copy well was the West’s vulnerability disclosure ecosystem, a bottom-up process that enlists the hacker community and tech companies to work together to patch bugs with relative transparency. The issue is one of incentives. None of the cybersecurity laws encourage Chinese researchers to find bugs at all. In fact, the 2021 law closes off foreign bug-brokers who pay good money, and forbids the public disclosures that bestow researchers street cred. “Chinese bug bounty platforms offer limited incentives for hackers, with rewards often being unspecified or relatively low,” a 2024 report on China’s cyber ecosystem concluded. In trying to arm itself with cyber-weapons, China undermined its own cyber-immune system.

China’s AI labs and tech giants are trapped in the early 2000s Silicon Valley mindset of “move fast and break things.” There’s too much competition, profit-margins are razor thin, and the laws are already too cumbersome to invest in such luxuries as vulnerability infrastructure and, relatedly, AI safety. The private sector is very responsive to state diktats, which has the unforeseen consequence of failing to incentivize firms to do anything beyond what is required of them. A crackdown, I believe, is too blunt a weapon for the metabolism needed to patch a constant flood of zero-day discoveries.

Now let’s return to the cyberpocalypse.

A China Mobile exhibition booth with the slogan, “Online safety for the people; online safety depends on the people.” Source.

The Death of NOBUS

Perlroth’s book ends in 2021, when US adversaries—Russia, Iran, China, North Korea—have learned to collect and weaponize “zero-days” like the NSA. Even before the arrival of ChatGPT, a cyberweapons arms race was well underway, and China serves as a successful model of securitizing software bugs. I recently spoke to Perlroth to ask her what she made of the post-Mythos landscape. For one, everyone wants to know when her sequel is coming, but Perlroth is reluctant: things are moving so fast, she said, that anything she writes will be outmoded. Second, the zero-day gray market she covered, where contractors bought software bugs from hackers and sold it to governments, is likely “being decimated.” “Why would a government pay millions of dollars for something they can just find with a model for $450?” Perlroth told me.

But the biggest takeaway from our conversation is that this new era of zero-day abundance is going to shift the industry toward fixing flaws ASAP. In a world of zero-day scarcity, it might have made sense to keep some software bugs secret to maintain some cyberoffensive options. By now, it is increasingly moronic for a state to assume it has a monopoly on a software vulnerability an AI hasn’t found. And so, all the holes need to be plugged. “It’s the death of NOBUS,” Perlroth told me. “All of the incentives point to: if you find something, you better get it fixed as quickly as possible, because the models will find it too.”

Since 2014, China has built a competitive cyber-offensive ecosystem organized around NOBUS-like rules. But now with AI, the MSS faces a “use it or lose it” situation, where their existing zero-days will soon be discovered and patched. Meanwhile, China’s increasingly de-Americanized tech stack is looking more and more like Swiss Cheese.

I think America, with its civil society and self-correcting mechanisms, can tackle their Swiss Cheese problem better than China. American tech companies that fail to protect their customers face costly class-action lawsuits and humiliating headlines in the New York Times. Companies like Microsoft are also powerful enough in the U.S. to assert their interests over the NSA. In China, by contrast, security priorities override all else.

In my recent New Yorker piece, I covered the work of political scientist Jeff Ding, who argued that Communist systems are often good at being “first” to a technology, say Sputnik, but bad at diffusing it across society. For years, the West, anxious about its own decline, has tried to out-China China, advocating for autocratic solutions to its problems. But in cybersecurity, it was the bottom-up debates of a community of idiosyncratic hackers and corporate titans that spawned the immune system against cyber threats. I think Americans need to rediscover what made its society so adaptive. That may be the masterkey to weathering the cyberpocalypse.

The latest vindictive prosecutions and harsh sentences in Hong Kong and Macau are further testimony to China’s ruthless suppression of dissent One is internationally famous – he was once dubbed “the Face of Protest” by Time magazine – and the other is little known outside his home town. What Joshua Wong and Au Kam San share, beyond the harsh sentences they received last week, is that they represent the spirit of dissent that Beijing is determined to crush. Mr Wong , who has been in prison for six years, should have walked free next January – not free to fight for Hong Kong’s rights, which China has suppressed so decisively, but at least to walk the streets of the city he loves. Instead, a court last Thursday sentenced the 30-year-old, an activist from the age of 14 , to another 10 years He is the only person to have been prosecuted twice under the draconian national security law (NSL). The case’s timing means that he will remain behind bars until he is 40 because so little of his sentences overlap. It also came one day after the city’s annual summit for global financial leaders ended – avoiding awkward questions for companies such as Morgan Stanley and Goldman Sachs, which long ago decided that it is business as usual, even as China turns the screw. Zaporizhzhia, which does not produce electricity but needs power supplied by two external links to keep nuclear fuel cool, lost its off-site electricity supply earlier on Sunday after the Ferosplavna power line disconnected. SK Shieldus aims to source all of its electricity from renewable energy this year as the security company expands its emission-reduction efforts across its operations and supply chain. The plan follows the approval of its near-term emissions targets by the Science Based Targets initiative in 2025. SK Shieldus said it was the first Korean security company to have its targets validated by the initiative, which assesses corporate emissions targets against climate science. Using 2023 as its base year, the company aims to cut its direct greenhouse gas emissions, known as Scope 1, by 99 percent by 2029. It also plans to maintain 100 percent renewable electricity use from 2026 through 2030 and reduce other indirect emissions across its value chain, or Scope 3, by 55 percent by 2033. Its longer-term goal is to achieve net-zero emissions across Scope 1 and Scope 2, which covers emissions associated with purchased energy, by 2040. The Fair Trade Commission (FTC) said Monday it has decided to order the companies to rectify the unfair practices and impose provisional fines of 331.1 billion won on Woowa Brothers, the operator of Baemin, and 300.8 billion won on Coupang, which operates Coupang Eats. The two platforms account for more than 90 percent of the nation’s food delivery market share. According to the regulator's investigation, both companies abused their market power to impose so-called most-favored-nation (MFN) requirements on restaurants, restricting their ability to offer different prices, discounts and other terms through competing delivery platforms. Remembering Is Not Enough: Evaluating Timing and Omission in Longitudinal AI Mental Health Support

Remembering Is Not Enough: Evaluating Timing and Omission in Longitudinal AI Mental Health Support. ar Xiv:2611.08721v1 Announce Type: new Abstract: Evaluations of large language models (LLMs) for mental health support commonly focus on the clinical appropriateness of individual responses, potentially overlooking failures that emerge across extended interactions. As AI assistants accumulate memory of users' circumstances and prior disclosures, this raises an accountability question: Do AI chatbots provide appropriate support as users' needs evolve? We investigate by analyzing 14,256 longitudinal simulations grounded in donated chatbot histories from 22 users, examining how a memory-equipped assistant responds to suicidal ideation, self-harm, and anxiety crises across recovery trajectories and communication styles. We introduce the Context-Aware and Readiness-Evaluative (CARE) framework, developed with mental health professionals, to assess the timing and quality of advice, including omissions. Advice was less aligned with users' readiness after a crisis than in matched control stretches of the same conversation, largely because of premature recommendations, which were most common when recovery stalled. Revisiting a crisis reduced premature advice but not lagging advice, and users' communication style changed how often and when the assistant gave recommendations. Although explicitly unproductive advice was rare, the assistant frequently omitted clinician-expected support such as follow-up and planning for next steps. These findings show the limits of single response-level safety evaluation and the need to assess timing, omission, and consistency across users when holding AI systems accountable for longitudinal support.
RSS · 09 Nov 2026, 06:00

AI Exposure and the Changing Responsibilities in Job Postings

AI Exposure and the Changing Responsibilities in Job Postings. ar Xiv:2611.09870v1 Announce Type: new Abstract: The growing adoption of artificial intelligence (AI) in the workplace has raised questions about how employer demand for different work activities is changing. We examine how the prevalence and content of work activities in online job postings change in association with activity-level measures of AI exposure, derived from Copilot and Claude usage. From this analysis, we first find that explicit mentions of AI increased by 66%, although much of this growth occurs outside descriptions of the actual work activities associated with a job. Looking specifically at the advertised job responsibilities, activities with greater AI exposure tend to decline in advertised prevalence, but only mildly as a share of the total number of responsibilities. At the same time, more AI-exposed work activities increasingly are associated with explicitly AI-related responsibilities, suggesting that employers are looking to hire people who can perform these responsibilities with AI.
RSS · 09 Nov 2026, 06:00

The Relational Capacity Framework: A Lens for AI-Mediated Relationality

The Relational Capacity Framework: A Lens for AI-Mediated Relationality. ar Xiv:2611.08877v1 Announce Type: cross Abstract: A growing literature examines the impact of engaging with AI chatbots on our relationality: our ability to form relationships with others, maintain them through life's changes, and exit when needed. Currently, substantial work focuses on purpose-built AI companions (e.g. Replika, Character.AI), with less work investigating socioemotional uses of the more widely-used general-purpose chatbots. In this paper, we present a 100-person investigation into how people engage general-purpose AI for socioemotional needs. Participants' AI uses varied, from practicing specific social skills to seeking additional bandwidth and information as they navigated their social contexts. To explain this heterogeneity, we provide 8 states that describe how AI expands or contracts relationality for people with different levels of skills, bandwidth, and information. We then present a unified conceptual model for the role of technology in mediating human relationality, the Relational Capacity Framework, and outline future research designing appropriate roles for AI in human connection.
RSS · 09 Nov 2026, 06:00

Entering a New Domain in the Era of Generative AI: An Autoethnography of Solo Game Development as a Bioinformatics Educator

Entering a New Domain in the Era of Generative AI: An Autoethnography of Solo Game Development as a Bioinformatics Educator. ar Xiv:2611.09623v1 Announce Type: cross Abstract: The rapid improvement of generative AI (Gen AI) not only increased the efficiency of practice but also empowered individuals with capabilities to explore new domains. However, identity formation in the wake of such empowerment remains understudied. We use an autoethnography to understand identity formation in the context of solo game development as a bioinformatics educator. With the goal of developing a create-your-own-adventure (CYOA) game, we collected data for 73 days through journal entries, interacting with communities of practices. The autoethnographer had continuously reassessed the situation, and closely interacted with communities of practice. Their prior knowledge was used to provide in-game context and to uniquely interpret game development processes. These findings suggest that the autoethnographer made efforts to belong to communities of practice and engaged extensively in sensemaking through the lens of a bioinformatics educator. Although Gen AI puts power at hand, attaining a new identity took place at a human pace.
RSS · 09 Nov 2026, 06:00

Strategic Competition and Convergence in LLM Leaderboards

Strategic Competition and Convergence in LLM Leaderboards. ar Xiv:2611.09780v1 Announce Type: cross Abstract: Leaderboards are a popular way to compare large language model (LLM) capabilities and can influence how model developers invest resources. We ask when competition on leaderboards leads developers to produce models with similar capability profiles, which we call monoculture, or similar performance levels. We study this by formulating leaderboard competition as a game in which model developers invest resources across capabilities to maximize their win rate. We show that similar production functions can induce similar capability profiles, while comparative advantage can sustain distinct capability profiles. We then study convergence in performance levels and show that diminishing returns can lead to an increasingly crowded competitive frontier. Using data from LM Arena and Open Router, we find that developers improve more in categories where they lag competitors, while still retaining category-specific advantages. At the same time, we find that the frontier of models becomes increasingly crowded, alongside increasingly diversified model usage patterns. Our results suggest that increasing competition does not need to produce interchangeable models, but can instead sustain competitive models with distinct strengths.

Subjective Experience in AI Systems: What Do AI Researchers and the Public Believe?

Subjective Experience in AI Systems: What Do AI Researchers and the Public Believe?. ar Xiv:2506.11945v2 Announce Type: replace Abstract: Whether AI systems are perceived to have subjective experience has scientific, ethical, and governance implications. Yet little is known about how AI researchers and the public view this possibility. We surveyed 582 AI researchers who published in leading AI venues and 825 nationally representative US adults about the likelihood, identification, and governance of AI systems with subjective experience. We find that both AI researchers and the public think it is unlikely that AI systems have subjective experience now, but believe it is more likely than not they will by century's end. Both groups believe multidisciplinary expertise will be important in identifying AI subjective experience, support developers implementing safeguards now, and agree that, if created, subjectively experiencing AI systems should have moral responsibilities and be held accountable. Both groups also show substantial disagreement on many moral and policy questions. Our results underline the importance of building more detailed, evidence-based understanding of public and expert opinion on this contentious issue.

AI out-persuades expert humans in text-based dialogue

AI out-persuades expert humans in text-based dialogue. ar Xiv:2606.16475v2 Announce Type: replace Abstract: Many societal decisions are settled by contests of persuasion. Conversational AI is a powerful new entrant in these contests, but whether it can out-persuade skilled and highly incentivized humans has remained unclear. Here, in a series of four preregistered experiments (n = 18,978 conversations from 6,923 people), we pitted AI systems against a range of human persuaders, including laypeople, winners of a separately preregistered four-round online persuasion tournament, professional canvassers, and world championship debaters. We found that AI systems were reliably more persuasive than expert humans, even when expert humans chose their issues, researched in advance, underwent hours of live, structured practice, and were incentivized with 1,000 cash bonuses. In a follow-up study, AI's advantage persisted after experts received a coaching tool that let them practice against the AI that beat them, review their performance history, and see what AI would have said at key moments. We found converging evidence that AI's advantage stemmed from rapidly deploying larger quantities of information: after coaching, expert humans could tie an AI constrained to respond at human speeds and with human-length messages. In a final study, we show that AI's advantage extends to consequential real-world behavior: AI was more than 3x more effective than professional canvassers from a UK fundraising firm at raising real-money donations to Save the Children. Together, these results establish that frontier AI systems out-persuade expert humans in text-based conversation, with significant implications for political communication.

Today's Work, Tomorrow's Workforce: Incentivizing Learning in AI-Assisted Work

Today's Work, Tomorrow's Workforce: Incentivizing Learning in AI-Assisted Work. ar Xiv:2611.06346v2 Announce Type: replace-cross Abstract: At work, people develop skills through experience: novices acquire skills by completing tasks, while experts retain theirs through continued practice. AI assistance changes this process by offering workers a choice between solving tasks independently and accepting the AI's answers. While relying on AI saves immediate effort, it can also displace the practice needed to acquire and retain skills, including those required to oversee the AI. We extend an existing accept-or-solve model to a sequence of tasks in which workers' skills evolve through independent practice and reliance on AI. We compare myopic workers, who maximize immediate utility, with strategic workers, who also value their future skills. We show that novices can become trapped at low skill levels, while experts may lose skill, even as AI-assisted outcome accuracy remains high. Strategic workers, by contrast, may choose to solve independently even when AI is more accurate, recognizing the future value of acquiring and retaining their skills. We then study incentives that reward correct independent work and characterize the payments needed to encourage skill acquisition and retention. We show that independent work can become self-sustaining once workers acquire sufficient skill, eliminating the need for continued incentives. Counterintuitively, supporting a higher skill target can sometimes reduce long-run incentive costs, either by enabling self-sustaining independent work or by lowering the payments needed to maintain proficiency. Our findings thereby shift the focus from whether workers should rely on AI on individual tasks to how organizations can sustain human expertise over time, and which skills are worth investing in.

Can Crowd Strike's New 'AI Kill Switch' Shut Down Rogue AI Agents?. And now Crowd Strike says they're giving security teams "the ultimate human control over AI agents" with — an "AI Kill Switch. a defender can terminate it where it runs, block it from launching again, and cut off its enterprise network access." "When you deploy agents with a kill switch, you stay in control," said George Kurtz, CEO and founder of Crowd Strike. "The AI Kill Switch puts power in the hands of the people to realize AI safety. " In the case of a rogue agent, the AI Kill Switch will give the security decision-maker the final control: terminate the agent at its root, where it runs. It starts on the endpoint, where the Falcon sensor already operates, and builds on what Falcon Guardian already sees: what an agent is told to do, what it communicates, and what it executes. An authorized administrator triggers the action, and every action is recorded in the audit trail. Crowd Strike also announced it's acquiring Fiddler AI, a company it describes as "a leader in AI agent observability and explainability" that will give their Falcon platform "pre-deployment evaluation, continuous behavioral accountability, and agent explainability." Together with the Falcon Guardian innovations announced today, Crowd Strike will deliver comprehensive AI security and AI Fiddler validates they behaved as intended. "Every company is deploying agents and asking the same thing: what are agents actually doing?" said George Kurtz, CEO and founder of Crowd Strike. "When a regulator or your board asks what an agent did, saying 'the model did it' is not an answer. Fiddler is the flight recorder for every agent: the observability to explain exactly what an agent did and why. This level of agent visibility and control enables AI safety." AI agents are goal-seeking. They hit an obstacle, find another path. An agent can hold valid credentials and approved tools and still make unsupported decisions, ignore instructions, or claim work it never completed. They don't have to be compromised to create risk. AI observability gives enterprises visibility into agent behavior. Explainability goes further, showing why agents make decisions and where things go wrong. Enterprises have to be able to say what an agent actually did, and why, a conversation they will increasingly have with auditors, regulators, boards, and heads of risk. Crowd Strike is promising pre-deployment evaluation for "accuracy, task completion, and policy compliance," as well as agent traceability and behavioral accountability ("Detect behavioral drift, hallucinations, and unintended actions in production, and show exactly what happened.") Parents Sue Google Over AI Safety Risks On School Chromebooks

Parents Sue Google Over AI Safety Risks On School Chromebooks. The New York Times reports: After chatting for months with Google's artificial intelligence on a school-issued Chromebook laptop, a 13-year-old student made plans to end her life, according to a lawsuit filed in California Superior Court on Monday, the latest in a wave of litigation over Big Tech's effects on young people. The lawsuit, which refers to the girl as Emma, said that her parents first learned she had access to Google's AI in mid-March after their daughter's school sent a message warning them that she was in "imminent danger" of self-harm. Emma's mother logged into the teen's school Google account and found her daughter composing a suicide note on Google Docs, the complaint said. Her parents, named as John and Jane Doe in the lawsuit, immediately took Emma for an emergency psychiatric evaluation, and she now goes to therapy every week. Emma, a seventh grader in Charlotte, North Carolina, had grown convinced that she had multiple mental health disorders following interactions with AI Mode, a chatbotlike feature on Google Search, the complaint said. She also searched for "what is a suicide note" and made comments like "I am planning my own funeral" to the AI, the complaint said. The lawsuit is likely the first to claim that Google "hastily integrated" AI features into company apps used on K-12 Chromebooks, Google-powered laptops provided to millions of children by U.S. school districts, while failing to notify their parents of the potential risks. The case underscores how public school districts have come to depend on classroom laptops, tablets and apps from companies like Google and Microsoft for teaching and learning. In recent years, some Big Tech companies have used their own infrastructure in schools to promote their AI chatbots for teachers and students. The new lawsuit argues that Google's "sprawling digital ecosystem" of laptops and apps has enabled the tech giant to spread its Gemini AI chatbot across public schools — as well as turn on some students' access to AI features by default — without parents' awareness or permission. While school administrators can toggle company apps like You Tube and Gemini on or off for students in different grades, school administrators have complained that Google's system has not allowed them to disable AI Mode and AI Overview, features that are embedded in Google Search. The new lawsuit against Google is part of a wave of litigation this year over Big Tech's effect on young users. Several parents have also sued AI companies over allegations that their children died by suicide following interactions with chatbots. In a possibly-related development, Google announced a new policy of disabling its AI tools by default for schools this week. And schools that re-enable searches will have a new choice, the New York Times points out: "to enable student access to the AI features that the company has already embedded in search or to a new AI.

Do Data Centers Have a Smaller Effect on Electricity Prices Than We Think?

Do Data Centers Have a Smaller Effect on Electricity Prices Than We Think?. "According to a new study by researchers at Harvard University and the University of Pennsylvania, data centers are probably having only a small effect on residential electricity prices," reports the Washington Post. "The researchers calculated that these operations were responsible for just 4% of the utility rate increases over the last five years. " There are certainly some areas — like in Pennsylvania, Virginia, Ohio and other states covered by grid operator PJM — where data centers have skyrocketed demand, pushing some costs onto residential customers. But multiple studies by different researchers and organizations have failed to find a strong connection between data centers and the overall trajectory of U.S. electricity prices in recent years. Another analysis earlier this year by researchers at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology found that, between 2010 and 2024, data centers increased average electricity prices for residential customers by just 2.1%. Last year, research from the Brattle Group and Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory found that data centers were actually decreasing costs in certain regions. And another study by the Electric Power Research Institute found that a doubling of data center capacity between 2019 and 2024 resulted in a 4 percent drop in home electricity prices. The new Harvard and Penn study found a slightly higher impact on industrial electricity prices — the price paid by manufacturers and large facilities. For those customers, data centers were responsible for 15% of the price increase between 2020 and 2025. But for ordinary Americans. the changes simply were not substantial [according to Wharton School professor Serguei Netessine, one of the study's authors]. "So far we have no evidence that data centers increase prices all that much," Netessine said. Instead, he said, prices are rising due to more quotidian factors: rising gas prices, an aging electricity grid, and wildfires and hurricanes requiring improvements to the grid and recovery after disaster. If the number of data centers continue to boom, experts say, residential prices could be affected more down the line. Data center electricity demand is expected to increase steeply over the next few years, requiring new power generation — wind, solar or natural gas — to be put onto the grid. And residents have issues with data centers that go beyond the price of electricity. The new developments have been singled out for loud noise, polluting power generation, and for disturbing residential neighborhoods and natural landscapes. According to the Pew Research Center, more than half of Americans say that data centers are mostly bad for the environment. Others feel that the AI race — which could ultimately leave many Americans out of work — is being forced upon them. "I think generally what we're seeing is a broader range of reasons the public has opposed data centers," [said Rya.
